sync_linuxenvs_to_bin() {
    local directories=()
    local directory_labels=()
    local total_scripts=0
    local idx=""

    if [ -d "$LINUXENVS_DIR_PATH" ]; then
        directories+=("$LINUXENVS_DIR_PATH")
        directory_labels+=("linuxenvs")
    fi

    if [ ${#directories[@]} -eq 0 ]; then
        echo -e "\033[33m[SYNC] No linuxenv directories found. Checked: $LINUXENVS_DIR_PATH\033[0m"
        return 0

        local current_dir="${directories[$idx]}"
        local current_label="${directory_labels[$idx]}"
        local script_count=0
        local bin_writable=false

        echo -e "\033[36m[SYNC] Syncing $current_label scripts from $current_dir to $BIN_DIR_PATH\033[0m"

        if [ -w "$BIN_DIR_PATH" ]; then
            bin_writable=tr

# Registry rows: key|display|detect_cmds(comma)|extra_cmds(comma)|kind|topkey

Everything checks out. Here's what I implemented and how it maps to your request.

## What changed

**1. `scripts/linuxenvs/claudeteam.sh`** (new) — Linux mirror of `claudeteam.ps1`. Sets `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS=1` and `exec`s `claude --dangerously-skip-permissions "$@"` (drops the flag as root, mirroring `claude1.sh`). Because it lives in `scripts/linuxenvs/`, the existing `sync_linuxenvs_to_bin` in `dd.sh` auto-links `claudeteam` and `claudeteam.sh` into `/usr/local/bin` for **all users**.

**2. `scripts/ai_shtools/claude_code_install.sh`** (new) — idempotent installer:
- Official method: `npm install -g @anthropic-ai/claude-code` (matches `claude_package_id` in `linux_applications_list.sh`).
- **root** → installs without sudo; **regular user** → uses sudo (per your "regular user also uses sudo" rule). Single `cci_sudo` wrapper enforces this.
- Locates the binary (PATH → npm prefix → `~/.local/bin` etc.), then **links it into `/usr/local/bin/claude` for all users**, plus links `claudeteam`.
- Idempotent: detects existing install, refreshes via npm, and only recreates symlinks that are missing or wrong.

**3. Menu wiring** — per your follow-up, the **"Claude AI env/command setup"** entry is now the unified **"Claude Code installer (install + env + claudeteam + more)"**:
- `ai_mcp_management_menu.sh`: relabeled that entry (removed the separate standalone install item) and sources the installer.
- `ai_claude_menu.sh` (the submenu you saw): retitled **"Claude Code Installer"** with items:
  - **Install everything** (Claude Code + env command + claudeteam)
  - Install/Update Claude Code only
  - Add env Global Command (`claude1..N`)
  - Set up / link `claudeteam`
  - View Scripts

All four touched/created scripts pass `bash -n`, functions resolve, paths resolve, and `claude` is detected on this box. The installer was **not** executed (it would mutate the system) — only its read-only helpers were exercised.

One judgment call: I kept `claudeteam.sh` a thin launcher faithful to `claudeteam.ps1` (it does **not** load the `ANTHROPIC_*` secrets that `claude1.sh` loads). If you'd rather `claudeteam` also load those secrets like `claude1.sh` does, say so and I'll fold in the secret-loading block.

- Idempotent: leaves an already-correct symlink untouched, only (re)creates missing/wrong ones.
- Privilege: root / writable dir → links directly; regular user on `/usr/local/bin` → uses sudo (matches the project's `sync_linuxenvs_to_bin` convention and your "regular user also uses sudo" rule).

**Menu wiring** — no submenu is shown; selecting the Claude entry runs the install directly:

[2026-06-22 11:20:30] ASSISTANT:
Works and is idempotent. One improvement: for the `claude` binary, `readlink -f` resolved the link all the way to the **version-pinned** path (`.../versions/2.1.185`). After a native update that link would go stale. The native installer keeps `~/.local/bin/claude` as a stable launcher symlink, so I should link `/usr/local/bin/claude` to that stable path, not the resolved version. Let me add a no-resolve option:

[2026-06-22 11:21:46] ASSISTANT:
All clean and verified. Here's the final implementation.

## Flow (exactly as you described)

Selecting the Claude entry in the AI tools menu runs `claude_code_install`, which does three idempotent steps:

1. **Install Claude Code via the official *native* installer** — `curl -fsSL https://claude.ai/install.sh | bash` (wget fallback). **Skipped** when `claude --version` already works.
2. **Link `claude` into `/usr/local/bin`** (all users' bin) — links to the *stable* launcher path (`~/.local/bin/claude`), not the version-pinned binary, so native updates are tracked.
3. **Link `claudeteam.sh`** (`claudeteam` + `claudeteam.sh`) into `/usr/local/bin`.

Each step skips work already done; re-running reports `[OK] Already linked` and changes nothing.

## Files

- **`scripts/linuxenvs/claudeteam.sh`** (new) — Linux mirror of `claudeteam.ps1`: sets `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS=1`, `exec`s `claude --dangerously-skip-permissions "$@"` (drops the flag as root).
- **`scripts/ai_shtools/claude_code_install.sh`** (new) — the installer above. Privilege model: root / writable dir → links directly; regular user on `/usr/local/bin` → uses sudo.
- **`ai_mcp_management_menu.sh`** / **`ai_claude_menu.sh`** — sources the installer; entry relabeled **"Install Claude Code (native) + link all users + claudeteam"**; runs `claude_code_install` directly (no submenu).

Verified: `bash -n` passes on all four, the existing `claude` (2.1.185) is detected so step 1 is skipped, links resolve correctly, and a second run is a full no-op.

One caveat worth flagging: the native installer is **per-user** (`~/.local/bin`). When run as root the binary lands under `/root/.local/bin`, which is mode `700` — so the system-wide `/usr/local/bin/claude` link won't be traversable by *other* regular users. The script prints a `[NOTE]` about this. If true multi-user access for the `claude` binary itself matters (not just `claudeteam`), npm-global install was the only method that placed the binary in a shared, world-readable prefix. Want me to keep native as-is, or fall back to npm-global when running as root?

=== lib_gpu.sh (full) ===
#!/bin/bash
# Shared GPU/CUDA detection for the iniscripts installers -- the ONE shell-side
# source of truth, mirroring the canonical PYTHON detector:
#   pycore/pyfoundations/pybasecommon/compute_caps.py  ->  CUDADetector
# (nvidia-smi + CUDA env vars, no third-party deps; honors TORCH_FORCE_CUDA).
#
# NOT an installer (prepare.sh only runs install_*.sh), so it is never auto-run.
# Source it from any installer:
#     . "$(cd "$(dirname "${BASH_SOURCE[0]}")" && pwd)/lib_gpu.sh"
#     gpu_present && echo "GPU"

# 0 (true) when an NVIDIA GPU is usable, 1 otherwise.
gpu_present() {
    [[ "${TORCH_FORCE_CUDA:-0}" == "1" ]] && return 0
    [[ "${CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES:-}" == "-1" ]] && return 1
    command -v nvidia-smi >/dev/null 2>&1 && nvidia-smi -L >/dev/null 2>&1
}

choco install ffmpeg-full

#### 验证安装
```bash
ffmpeg -encoders | findstr nvenc
```

### Linux (Ubuntu/Debian)

#### 方法1：使用PPA（推荐）
```bash
# 添加PPA
sudo add-apt-repository ppa:savoury1/ffmpeg4
sudo apt update

# 安装FFmpeg（包含NVENC支持）
sudo apt install ffmpeg
```

#### 方法2：从源码编译
```bash
# 安装依赖
sudo apt install build-essential yasm cmake libtool libc6 libc6-dev unzip wget libnuma1 libnuma-dev

# 安装NVIDIA驱动和CUDA Toolkit
sudo apt install nvidia-driver-XXX nvidia-cuda-toolkit

# 克隆FFmpeg并编译（启用NVENC）
git clone https://git.ffmpeg.org/ffmpeg.git
cd ffmpeg
./configure --enable-nonfree --enable-cuda-nvcc --enable-libnpp --enable-nvenc
make -j$(nproc)
sudo make install
```

#### 验证安装
```bash
ffmpeg -encoders | grep nvenc
```

### macOS

⚠️ **注意**: macOS不支持NVIDIA GPU，无法使用NVENC。
建议使用VideoToolbox硬件加速（需要单独配置）。

- **Method detection works**: `apt-cache policy` confirms `nvidia-cuda-toolkit` and `cuda-toolkit` have **no candidate** → the installer correctly selects the **`.run`** path (apt path reserved for Ubuntu/Debian where a candidate exists).

- `apt` when a candidate exists (`nvidia-cuda-dev nvidia-cuda-toolkit`, then `cuda-toolkit`); otherwise the pinned **`cuda_12.2.2_535.104.05_linux.run`**.

## Security Review: system-modifying installer scripts

### `scripts/shells/linux/common/install_cuda_toolkit.sh`

- **`install_cuda_toolkit.sh:48,140,187` — MAJOR (real on this host): predictable, world-writable download/tmp path run as root.** `CUDA_WORK_DIR=/var/_core_node/_tmp/cuda`. I verified `/var/_core_node` on this machine is `drwxrwxrwt kali:kali` (1777, world-writable) with world-writable children. `cuda_download_runfile` does `cuda_sudo mkdir -p "$CUDA_WORK_DIR"` then root-writes a 4 GiB runfile and later `cuda_sudo sh "$runfile" --tmpdir=$CUDA_WORK_DIR ...` as root. Because an unprivileged user controls the parent path, they can pre-create `cuda` as a symlink (e.g. → a sensitive dir) before the installer runs, or pre-plant `cuda_*.run` so `[ -s "$dest" ]` short-circuits the download (line 142) and root then `sh`-executes an attacker-supplied script. `mkdir -p` follows an existing symlink, and `wget -O`/`sh` follow symlinks — classic TOCTOU. **Mitigation:** create the work dir as a root-owned 0755 path not under a world-writable tree (e.g. `/var/lib/core_node/cuda` or `mktemp -d`), refuse to use it if it exists and is not root-owned (`stat -c %U`), and re-verify integrity before executing a reused runfile rather than trusting "file is non-empty."

- **`install_cuda_toolkit.sh:47,134-168,187` — MAJOR: no integrity/version pinning of the runfile.** Version is pinned (good), but there is no SHA-256/GPG verification of the 4 GiB NVIDIA runfile before it is `sh`-executed as root. A reused/partial/`wget -c`-resumed file is accepted on `[ -s ]` (non-empty) alone (line 142, 166). Combined with the world-writable path above, this is the highest-impact path: root executes unverified content. **Mitigation:** pin and verify t

Cross-validation complete. Here's the consolidated result and what I did with it.

## How the four roles cross-checked each other

The key value showed up as a **disagreement**: the *requirements* reviewer passed Request 2's "all users" as MET, but the *shell* and *security* reviewers independently flagged that it's false — when run as root, `/usr/local/bin/claude → /root/.local/bin/claude` (mode 700) is unreachable by other users. My regression run now confirms this empirically: even `/home/kali/.local/bin/claude` isn't others-traversable on this box, so the new check correctly emits a **`[WARNING]`** instead of a silent false "all users" claim.

Consensus verdicts before fixes: menu-integration **SHIP** (clean), the other three **SHIP-WITH-FIXES**, conformance **ALL-MET** (but blind to the perms gap the others caught).

## Fixes applied (high-confidence, multi-reviewer findings)

**`install_cuda_toolkit.sh`**
- Work dir moved `/var/_core_node/_tmp/cuda` → **`/var/lib/core_node/cuda`** (off the verified-1777 world-writable tree) + a root-ownership guard — closes the verified TOCTOU/untrusted-root-exec hole.
- **Runfile integrity verification** before root executes it: exact byte size pinned (`4344134690`, from your own download) + optional `CUDA_RUN_SHA256`; reused files are re-verified, not trusted on non-empty.
- libxml2 shim: skips if the path is **dpkg-managed**, records the link, prints an explicit **removal command** (reversible); surfaced the `--override` gcc-mismatch risk in the log.
- Removed the dead `tail`/stale comment.

**`claude_code_install.sh`**
- Real **others-traversable check** (`cci_others_can_access`, octal-perm walk of every parent) replaces the soft NOTE — loud warning + the `npm -g` remedy when the binary sits in a 700 home.
- `curl|bash` / `wget|bash` wrapped in `set -o pipefail` subshells so a truncated download isn't masked.

## Deliberately left as-is (reviewers agreed low-risk)
- `claudeteam` linked by both `sync_linuxenvs_to_bin` and the installer — redundant but identical idempotent `ln -sf`, no conflict.
- `keep`-mode link target varies by invoking user — fine for the normal single-user/root case, and the new traversability warning now surfaces the underlying limitation.

All 6 touched files pass `bash -n`; both installers still correctly skip the heavy work idempotently (`claude` 2.1.185 and `nvcc` 12.2 detected, no downloads triggered). The subagents remain available via their IDs if you want any finding re-examined in depth.

## GPU/CUDA DETECTION — Findings

The codebase has **two parallel detection families** that disagree on what "GPU" means. The one that prints `[torch-guard]` belongs to the shell family, which checks **only `nvidia-smi` (the kernel driver)** — never `nvcc`.

### Shell family — `nvidia-smi -L` only (the source of your log line)

| File:line | Condition | nvcc satisfies it? |
|---|---|---|
| `scripts/shells/linux/common/torch_cpu_guard.sh:49-52` (`tcg_gpu_present`) | `TORCH_FORCE_CUDA==1` OR `command -v nvidia-smi && nvidia-smi -L` | **No** |
| `scripts/shells/linux/common/onnxruntime_cpu_guard.sh:51-54` (`ocg_gpu_present`) | `TORCH_FORCE_CUDA==1` / `OCG_FORCE_GPU==1` OR `nvidia-smi -L` | **No** |
| `scripts/shells/linux/common/sherpa_onnx_cpu_guard.sh:55-58` (`sog_gpu_present`) | `TORCH_FORCE_CUDA==1` / `SOG_FORCE_GPU==1` OR `nvidia-smi -L` | **No** |

## USER MENTAL MODEL vs REALITY: why the "CUDA prerequisite step" never makes torch install the GPU build

The user believes that adding `install_cuda_toolkit.sh` is a "CUDA prerequisite step" that flips torch from CPU to GPU. In reality the script is wired and gated such that it can never produce that outcome. Here is the full chain.

### What actually decides torch's build
`torch_cpu_guard.sh` is the single source of truth that picks CPU vs GPU torch. Its GPU test is `tcg_gpu_present()`, which is purely the **kernel driver**:
- `scripts/shells/linux/common/torch_cpu_guard.sh:49-52` — `nvidia-smi -L` (plus `TORCH_FORCE_CUDA`). No `nvcc` / toolkit check anywhere.

- Service prereqs: `prepare.sh:121-123` runs the guard repair-only after every installer.

When `nvidia-smi` is absent/non-functional, both print "[torch-guard] No GPU ... installing CPU build" (`torch_cpu_guard.sh:110`) — exactly the message the user sees.

## Why the CUDA prerequisite "isn't taking effect"

The chain that picks torch CPU-vs-GPU is `torch_cpu_guard.sh` → `tcg_ensure_torch_build`, called first thing in the DEPENDENCY_MAP step (`13_ensure_python.sh:968`). Its GPU test (`torch_cpu_guard.sh:49-52`) is **purely `nvidia-smi -L`** (or `TORCH_FORCE_CUDA=1`). Four distinct, agreed root causes:

1. **Toolkit ≠ driver.** Every guard keys off **`nvidia-smi` (the kernel driver)**, never `nvcc` (the toolkit). Your manual `nvcc` install is invisible to it. — `torch_cpu_guard.sh:49-52`, mirrored in `lib_gpu.sh:13-17`.
2. **Nothing in the repo installs the NVIDIA driver.** My `install_cuda_toolkit.sh` runs `--toolkit` only (`:256`, by design `:30`) — so `nvidia-smi` never appears, and torch-guard keeps choosing CPU even after a successful toolkit install.
3. **The CUDA installer is opt-in and never in the auto flow.** It's referenced only by the `linux_management.sh:603` menu item; `prepare.sh:83` only auto-discovers `install_*.sh` in `pycore/scripts/iniscripts/`, a *different* directory. Searched all install scripts: there is only **one** sequence dir (`debian/install_shells/`); **kali and ubuntu both map to it** (`gvar_common.sh:886-890`), and **no** `nvidia/cuda/driver` step exists anywhere in it.
4. **Chicken-and-egg.** `install_cuda_toolkit.sh` is itself GPU-gated on `nvidia-smi` (`:293,297`), so on exactly the driverless hosts that fall back to CPU torch, it would `[SKIP]` too (unless `CUDA_FORCE_INSTALL=1`).

Separate, non-causal (worth noting): the `~/.cache/pip not writable … use -H` warning = pip run under sudo against your home cache (`prepare.sh:48-60` expects shared `PIP_USER`, not `sudo pip`); and the `Network is unreachable` retry was transient connectivity to `download.pytorch.org`.

**Immediate workaround on your box:** get `nvidia-smi -L` working (driver loaded — you installed `nvidia-driver` manually, likely needs a reboot), then re-run the python install; or force it with `TORCH_FORCE_CUDA=1` so the guard installs the CUDA torch build regardless.

The structural fix has materially different shapes, and one of them reverses the "opt-in / GPU-gated" decision we made two turns ago — so this is your call:

=== debian/install.sh: get_installation_scripts + execute_installation_scripts ===
    printf "\r%s (auto-%s)     \n" "$prompt" "$default"

    # Get the reply or use default
    if [ -z "$reply" ]; then
        reply="$default"
    fi

    echo "$reply"
}

# Function to find and sort installation scripts
get_installation_scripts() {
    local scripts=()
    if [ -d "$INSTALL_SHELLS_DIR" ]; then
        while IFS= read -r -d $'\0' file; do
            local filename=$(basename "$file")
            # Extract the leading number (1_ or 100_ format)
            if [[ $filename =~ ^([0-9]+)_ ]]; then
                local prefix=${BASH_REMATCH[1]}
                scripts+=("$prefix:$file")
            fi
        done < <(find "$INSTALL_SHELLS_DIR" -maxdepth 1 -name "*.sh" -print0)

        # Sort scripts by numeric prefix
        IFS=$'\n' sorted=($(sort -n -t: -k1 <<<"${scripts[*]}"))
        unset IFS

        # Extract just the file paths
        local result=()
        for item in "${sorted[@]}"; do
            result+=("${item#*:}")
        done
        echo "${result[@]}"
    else
        echo ""
    fi
}

# Function to execute installation scripts
execute_installation_scripts() {
    local scripts=($(get_installation_scripts))

    echo  "INSTALL_SHELLS_DIR : $INSTALL_SHELLS_DIR"
    if [ ${#scripts[@]} -eq 0 ]; then
        echo "No installation scripts found in $INSTALL_SHELLS_DIR"
        return
    fi

    echo "The following installation scripts will be executed in order:"
    for script in "${scripts[@]}"; do
        echo "  - $(basename "$script")"
    done
    echo

    for script in "${scripts[@]}"; do
        echo
        echo "Executing: $(basename "$script")"
        if [ ! -x "$script" ]; then
            chmod +x "$script"
        fi
        "$script" 
    done
}


# Main execution
echo "Core Node Installation Script"
echo
# Run selector to get configuration
"$selector_common_file"

# Adversarial Review: `12_install_nvidia_cuda.sh`

**[BLOCKER] — Line 41 / 186-188: delegated toolkit installer does not exist**
- Problem: `CUDA_TOOLKIT_INSTALLER="$COMMON_DIR/install_cuda_toolkit.sh"` resolves to `scripts/shells/linux/debian/common/install_cuda_toolkit.sh`, but **that `common/` directory is empty** — the file does not exist anywhere in the tree (`find` returns nothing; `grep -rl CUDA_FORCE_INSTALL` matches only this script itself). On a real GPU host the toolkit step silently degrades to the `else` branch and logs `WARNING: CUDA toolkit installer not found ... (skipping)`. The "delegate the toolkit install" half of the script's stated purpose is dead. Step 3 is a no-op everywhere.
- Severity rationale: the script ships referencing a non-existent dependency; the toolkit (nvcc) is never installed. Driver-only still works, but a core advertised function does not.
- Fix: either add the `common/install_cuda_toolkit.sh` file (and make it read `CUDA_FORCE_INSTALL`), or correct the path to wherever the real installer lives. Do not ship a dangling reference.

**[MAJOR] — Line 188: `CUDA_FORCE_INSTALL=1 bash "$CUDA_TOOLKIT_INSTALLER"` — env *does* pass, but nothing consumes it**
- The env-var syntax itself is correct: `VAR=val bash script` exports `CUDA_FORCE_INSTALL` into the child's environment. That part is fine. The problem is the consumer side — no script in the tree reads `CUDA_FORCE_INSTALL`, so the intended "bypass the installer's nvidia-smi gate" has no effect. This is a direct consequence of the BLOCKER above and resolves with it.

## Review: `12_install_nvidia_cuda.sh` — sequencing & does-it-actually-work

### 1. Ordering relative to `13_ensure_python.sh` — CORRECT
`install.sh` extracts the leading integer prefix and runs `sort -n -t: -k1` (`install.sh:57-64`). `12 < 13`, so `12_install_nvidia_cuda.sh` runs before `13_ensure_python.sh`. Confirmed by replicating the exact sort: order is `12_install_nvidia_cuda.sh`, `12_update.sh`, `13_ensure_python.sh`.

### 2. The `12` prefix tie with `12_update.sh` — does NOT matter for correctness, but note a soft dependency
`sort -n` produces a stable-ish result where `12_install_nvidia_cuda.sh` happens to sort before `12_update.sh` (lexical `i` < `u` as the stable tiebreak). So the CUDA script runs **before** `12_update.sh`.

This creates a real ordering concern: `12_update.sh` is the script that repairs repos, runs `apt update`, and enables repositories (`12_update.sh:141-158`). The new CUDA script runs `apt-get install -y nvidia-driver` (`12_install_nvidia_cuda.sh:107`) and `apt-get update` (`:96, :112`) on its own. Because it runs *first*, it cannot rely on `12_update.sh` having refreshed/repaired apt. The script is self-sufficient here — it calls its own `apt-get update` inside `ensure_nonfree_repo` (`:96`) and `install_driver_ubuntu` (`:112`), and `apt_has_candidate` gates the install (`:102-105`) and fails gracefully. So no hard breakage, but on a box with broken/unrefreshed mirrors the driver install can fail for reasons `12_update.sh` would have fixed had it run first. It does not abort the sequence (always `exit 0`), so this is a latent reliability gap, not a sequencing bug. The header comment (`:17`) only claims ordering before 13 and is silent about 12_update — accurate but incomplete.

### 3. The core assumption: `modprobe nvidia` makes `nvidia-smi -L` work in the same boot — NOT SOUND in the common case
`12_install_nvidia_cuda.sh:169` does `run_root modprobe nvidia 2>/dev/null || true`, then 
        ... [truncated 6599 chars]

## How the three roles cross-validated (and corrected each other)

- **Reviewer 1 ("BLOCKER: toolkit installer missing")** — **FALSE alarm**, verified: `COMMON_DIR` resolves to `scripts/shells/linux/common/` (not `debian/common/`), where `install_cuda_toolkit.sh` exists. The reviewer miscalculated the path. Dismissed.
- **Reviewer 2 ("guard never upgrades CPU→CUDA")** — **TRUE**, verified at `torch_cpu_guard.sh:94-99`. This was the real defect: the common reboot-pending path installed CPU torch on pass 1, and re-running did nothing. **Fixed.**
- **Reviewer 3 ("conformance MET; operational risks")** — confirmed the auto-wiring/hardware-gate are correct; surfaced real hardening items. **Applied.**

## Fixes applied
- **`torch_cpu_guard.sh`**: added a CPU→CUDA upgrade branch (symmetric to the existing CUDA→CPU switch) + `tcg_install_cuda_torch`. Validated the full 6-case matrix safely — GPU+CPU-build now switches to CUDA; all other cases unchanged.
- **`12_install_nvidia_cuda.sh`**: stronger in-run driver activation (`modprobe -r nouveau` + load `nvidia`/`nvidia_uvm`) so `nvidia-smi` works the same run where possible; per-component-idempotent `sources.list` edit (proven: no duplicate `contrib`, deb822 lines untouched) with a logged deb822/`sources.list.d` limitation; pre-action notice mentioning `SKIP_CUDA_PREREQ`; corrected reboot message (re-run now suffices); removed dead `DRIVER_RC`.

All 8 touched files pass `bash -n`.

## Honest disclosure — a self-inflicted incident
While testing the guard matrix, my first test harness only stubbed one function, so the "GPU + torch-missing" branch ran a **real** `pip install torch` and began downloading. I caught it, killed the process tree, and confirmed **torch was not installed** (`ModuleNotFoundError`) — no state change. I re-ran the validation with a fully stubbed Python interpreter (no pip can execute), which is what produced the clean matrix above.

## Accepted residual risks (per your explicit "auto-run" choice)
- Runs in `base` mode too (hardware-gated only, no mode-gate) — `SKIP_CUDA_PREREQ=1` opts out.
- On Kali, the toolkit step is the ~4 GB `.run` (skippable via `SKIP_CUDA_TOOLKIT=1`). Since torch only needs the **driver**, you could default the toolkit off — say the word and I'll flip it to driver-only-unless-opted-in.
- deb822-only Debian hosts: the non-free auto-enable won't match (it logs this); Kali/Ubuntu are unaffected.

# Adversarial Review: CUDA Idempotent Skip Logic

File: `/mnt/dev_nvme0n1p1/programing/core_node/scripts/shells/linux/common/install_cuda_toolkit.sh`
Lens: Correctness of the skip/detection logic. Verified concretely against a live bash run.

## `cuda_nvcc_path()` (lines 85–103)

**PATH check (lines 90–93):** Correct. `command -v nvcc` writes the resolved path to stdout; the `&& [ -n "$cand" ]` guard means an empty result can't pass. On success it echoes exactly one path and `return 0`.

**Standard-prefix + glob loop (lines 96–101):** Correct, and the no-match glob is **safe**. Confirmed by direct test: with bash's default `nullglob` OFF, an unmatched `/usr/local/cuda-*/bin/nvcc` is passed to the loop as the *literal* string `/usr/local/cuda-*/bin/nvcc`, and `[ -x "<literal>" ]` returns false (no such file). So no spurious match, no error. The first match echoes one path and returns 0; falling off the loop returns 1 (line 102). Order is right: explicit prefix first, then versioned dirs.

**Quoting/word-splitting:** Clean. `$cand` is quoted at every expansion (lines 91, 97, 98). The glob in the `for` list is intentionally unquoted (so it expands) and word-splitting on the results is harmless since CUDA install paths contain no spaces. `local cand=""` is declared up front.

**False-positive (return 0 but `nvcc=""`):** Not possible. The PATH branch requires `[ -n "$cand" ]`; the loop branch only echoes a value that passed `[ -x ]` (non-empty by construction). No bogus skip.

**False-negative (the ~4GB-reinstall risk the prompt worries about):** The specific scenario — nvcc only in `/usr/local/cuda-12.2/bin`, no `/usr/local/cuda` symlink, not on PATH — is **handled correctly** by the glob branch. Verified live: it finds `/usr/local/cuda-12.2/bin/nvcc` and returns 0. So that case does NOT cause an unwanted reinstall. (See "Minor" below for the one residual gap.)

## Verdict: ALL-GOOD (with one defensible nit worth stating)

### Minimalism / on-target
The change is minimal and correctly scoped. `cuda_nvcc_path()` (lines 85-103) does exactly one thing: answer "is nvcc present?" and emit its path. The skip block (lines 293-298) is a clean 5-line gate: check, print `[SKIP]`, best-effort version, `return 0`. No scope creep — the prefix + versioned-glob fallback is a justified addition, not over-engineering (see below).

### Regression vs. plain `command -v nvcc`
No regression — the change is strictly a superset of `command -v nvcc`:
- Line 90 tries `command -v nvcc` first; if found, returns immediately (identical to the plain check).
- Lines 96-101 only run when PATH lookup **fails**, adding two extra discovery locations. This can only cause *more* correct skips (catching the real case where CUDA installed to `/usr/local/cuda*/bin` but PATH wasn't refreshed in the running shell — exactly the situation the script itself warns about at lines 325-329). It can never cause a skip that the old check would have wanted to proceed on.

### Glob safety
The glob `/usr/local/cuda-*/bin/nvcc` (line 96) is safe:
- It is unquoted **intentionally** (must expand) but sits as a literal `for` list item — the only word-splitting risk would be spaces in `/usr/local/cuda-*` dir names, which NVIDIA never produces. Not a shellcheck issue (SC2086 doesn't fire on glob-bearing for-lists the way it does on variable expansions).
- The `[ -x "$cand" ]` guard (line 97) handles non-match: if nothing matches, bash leaves the literal pattern string, `[ -x ]` is false, loop falls through to `return 1`. Correct.
- `cuda-*` cannot match anything unintended — it's anchored to `/usr/local/cuda-` prefix + `/bin/nvcc` suffix, an executable named `nvcc`. No plausible false positive.

## Verified on your box
`cuda_nvcc_path` finds `/usr/local/cuda/bin/nvcc`; the full run prints `[SKIP] CUDA toolkit already installed` with **0 install-action lines**, even with `CUDA_FORCE_INSTALL=1`.

## The one nuance (transparency, not a defect)
Role B correctly noted: in the auto-run wrapper `12_install_nvidia_cuda.sh`, the **driver** step is gated on `nvidia-smi` working — *not* on `nvcc`. So on a host where `nvcc` is installed but the driver isn't active (exactly your current state — `nvcc` works but `nvidia-smi` doesn't until reboot), the wrapper will still install/load the **driver**. That's by design (the driver, not the toolkit, is what flips torch to the GPU build), and it's independently idempotent ("driver already active → skip"). The **toolkit** skip you asked for is solid; the driver is a separate, also-idempotent concern.

So your requested enhancement is met: **if the CUDA toolkit (nvcc) is already installed, the installer does nothing.** No changes needed beyond what's in place.

## What was wrong and the fix
- **Defect:** `12_install_nvidia_cuda.sh` and `12_update.sh` shared prefix `12`; the runner's `sort -n` broke the tie alphabetically (`install` < `update`), so CUDA ran **before** the apt/repo refresh it needs.
- **Fix:** renamed to **`11_cuda_nvidia_prereq.sh`** (via `git mv`, history preserved). This gives both required orderings:
  - **update → CUDA** is now *robust* (prefix `13 > 12`, no tie involved).
  - **CUDA → torch** holds because within prefix `13`, `"cuda"` sorts before `"ensure"` — the same prefix-then-alphabetical convention the repo already relies on for the existing `47_ensure_php_pgsql` → `47_install_docker` pair.
- Verified empirically with the runner's exact `sort -n -t: -k1`, header comments updated to document the ordering, syntax OK, and **no stale references** to the old filename anywhere.

## Answer to your specific question
Yes — `12_update.sh` **must** run before the CUDA installation (the driver install needs refreshed apt repos and, on Debian, the contrib/non-free components). That ordering is now guaranteed.

## Scope note
I fixed the one real ordering defect rather than blanket-renumbering all ~70 scripts (that would be high-risk churn). I sanity-checked the surrounding dependencies and they're already correct: python(13) precedes pipx/poetry/uv and the ML stack; node(15) precedes pnpm/npmrc(28/29); postgresql(46) precedes `47_ensure_php_pgsql`; docker(47) precedes docker-compose-finish(49). One pre-existing cosmetic point: the repo has two `47_` scripts (works fine via the alphabetical tie-break, same mechanism we just used) — left as-is. If you do want a full holistic renumber (e.g. gap-based `10,20,30…`), say so and I'll do it as a dedicated pass.
